Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, to be perfectly honest, it’s my sleep. It has become completely unmoored. Just utterly untethered from reality. At first, I actually thought it was a good thing, you know? I’ve been out of the workforce for a while now, but suddenly, a few weeks ago, I was waking up after only two or three hours of sleep, and I wasn't tired in the slightest. I felt incredibly sharp, just completely ready to go. I’d get out of bed at two or three in the morning, go straight to my study, and just start drafting these elaborate plans—pulling out my old graduate school notes, outlining huge projects, writing pages and pages until the sun came up. I felt like I had this incredible new lease on life, to be frank. 

But... well, that’s not what’s happening now. It’s shifted into something else entirely. Now, I still can't sleep, but that productive energy is completely gone. I just lie awake in bed, hours before dawn, and my stomach is so tight, just tied in these horrible knots. I toss and I turn, and there is absolutely no sense of rest whatsoever. I’m physically exhausted, bone-tired really, but I just lie there in the dark feeling completely wretched and tense. It's just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore, and the nights just feel impossibly long.

Depressed? Well, you know, doctor, it's interesting you ask about that timeframe specifically, because when I think back to the months before this whole... explosion of energy happened, it's almost hard to reconcile the two periods. But depressed most of the day, nearly every day? No, I wouldn't say that. Not at all, really. 

I mean, certainly, being out of the workforce at my age, having stepped away from the daily grind and the intellectual stimulation of my career, there are moments of quiet. There are afternoons where the house feels a bit too still, or days where I might wonder if I retired too early, or if I should have taken on that consulting role they offered me a few years back. You know, you spend decades building a career, pouring your intellect into complex problems, and then suddenly you're just... tending to the garden or reading the paper. It's an adjustment, undoubtedly. There were days where I felt perhaps a bit aimless, or maybe just bored, if I'm being perfectly frank. But a deep, pervasive depression? No. 

I wasn't spending my days crying or unable to get out of bed, nothing like that. I was still going to my weekly lunch with my old colleagues—well, most weeks anyway, unless the weather was terrible or my arthritis was acting up. I was still keeping up with the financial news, reading my journals, doing the crossword. I was functioning perfectly fine, just perhaps living a quieter, less vibrant life than I used to. So, no, I wouldn't classify my mood before this recent... episode as depressed. It was just an ordinary, perhaps slightly mundane, retirement existence before everything suddenly shifted into high gear.

Two weeks? Hmm. You know, doctor, looking back over the entire span of my life... and at sixty-nine, there's quite a bit of life to look back on, isn't there? I mean, I spent over forty years in academia and consulting, which is an incredibly demanding environment, just constantly pushing yourself, constantly under pressure to publish, to secure grants, to advise graduate students who were often in the middle of their own crises. It was a pressure cooker, frankly. And of course, there were times during all those years when things were difficult. When a major project would fall through, or when my late wife and I were first married and struggling to make ends meet while I finished my dissertation—those were stressful times. Very stressful. 

But a period of two straight weeks where I felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day? Where it just blanketed everything? I really... I don't think so. I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.

At first, I actually thought it was a good thing, you know? I’ve been out of the workforce for a while now, but suddenly, a few weeks ago, I was waking up after only two or three hours of sleep, and I wasn't tired in the slightest. I felt incredibly sharp, just completely ready to go. I’d get out of bed at two or three in the morning, go straight to my study, and just start drafting these elaborate plans—pulling out my old graduate school notes, outlining huge projects, writing pages and pages until the sun came up. I felt like I had this incredible new lease on life, to be frank. \n\nBut... well, that’s not what’s happening now. It’s shifted into something else entirely. Now, I still can't sleep, but that productive energy is completely gone. I just lie awake in bed, hours before dawn, and my stomach is so tight, just tied in these horrible knots. I toss and I turn, and there is absolutely no sense of rest whatsoever. I’m physically exhausted, bone-tired really, but I just lie there in the dark feeling completely wretched and tense. It's just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore, and the nights just feel impossibly long.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually powerful, gifted, or important?\n\nPatient: Powerful? Gifted? Well, it’s funny you use those exact words, doctor, because... yes. Absolutely. I mean, sitting here in this chair right now, exhausted and miserable, it sounds completely absurd, but at the time, the clarity I possessed was just staggering. It was as if a fog I didn't even know was there had completely lifted. \n\nI would be sitting in my study at three in the morning, looking over my portfolios, and I truly believed—no, I *knew*—that I could single-handedly repair my retirement accounts, which, let me tell you, took quite a hit over the last few years with all the market fluctuations. I saw patterns in the market that I thought everyone else was just too blind to see. But it wasn't just my own finances. I had this absolute certainty that I was the missing piece for other people, too. I started drafting these incredibly lengthy emails to three different tech start-ups—companies I have absolutely no prior relationship with, mind you, just places I had read about—convinced I was the only person alive who could properly advise them on their expansion strategies. \n\nAnd the local arts council! I've been a passive, quiet donor for a decade, never really involved, but suddenly I had this grand, sweeping vision to turn the entire organization into a regional powerhouse. I was drawing up organizational charts, drafting new mission statements, completely restructuring their board of directors on my legal pads. I felt... well, I felt as though my judgment was suddenly, undeniably superior to everyone else’s. Like I had tapped into this well of sheer genius that had just been sitting dormant since my graduate school days, and I was the only one who could see how all the pieces of the world were supposed to fit together. I was going to fix everything, doctor, just completely revolutionize all of it...\n\nDoctor: Before this energized period began, had you ever gone through a stretch where you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, you know, doctor, it's interesting you ask about that timeframe specifically, because when I think back to the months before this whole... explosion of energy happened, it's almost hard to reconcile the two periods. But depressed most of the day, nearly every day? No, I wouldn't say that. Not at all, really. \n\nI mean, certainly, being out of the workforce at my age, having stepped away from the daily grind and the intellectual stimulation of my career, there are moments of quiet. There are afternoons where the house feels a bit too still, or days where I might wonder if I retired too early, or if I should have taken on that consulting role they offered me a few years back. You know, you spend decades building a career, pouring your intellect into complex problems, and then suddenly you're just... tending to the garden or reading the paper. It's an adjustment, undoubtedly. There were days where I felt perhaps a bit aimless, or maybe just bored, if I'm being perfectly frank. But a deep, pervasive depression? No. \n\nI wasn't spending my days crying or unable to get out of bed, nothing like that. I was still going to my weekly lunch with my old colleagues—well, most weeks anyway, unless the weather was terrible or my arthritis was acting up. I was still keeping up with the financial news, reading my journals, doing the crossword. I was functioning perfectly fine, just perhaps living a quieter, less vibrant life than I used to. So, no, I wouldn't classify my mood before this recent... episode as depressed. It was just an ordinary, perhaps slightly mundane, retirement existence before everything suddenly shifted into high gear.\n\nDoctor: At any other time in your life, have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Hmm. You know, doctor, looking back over the entire span of my life... and at sixty-nine, there's quite a bit of life to look back on, isn't there? I mean, I spent over forty years in academia and consulting, which is an incredibly demanding environment, just constantly pushing yourself, constantly under pressure to publish, to secure grants, to advise graduate students who were often in the middle of their own crises. It was a pressure cooker, frankly. And of course, there were times during all those years when things were difficult. When a major project would fall through, or when my late wife and I were first married and struggling to make ends meet while I finished my dissertation—those were stressful times. Very stressful. \n\nBut a period of two straight weeks where I felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day? Where it just blanketed everything? I really... I don't think so. I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I was still keeping up with the financial news, reading my journals, doing the crossword. I was functioning perfectly fine, just perhaps living a quieter, less vibrant life than I used to. So, no, I wouldn't classify my mood before this recent... episode as depressed. It was just an ordinary, perhaps slightly mundane, retirement existence before everything suddenly shifted into high gear.\n\nDoctor: At any other time in your life, have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Hmm. You know, doctor, looking back over the entire span of my life... and at sixty-nine, there's quite a bit of life to look back on, isn't there? I mean, I spent over forty years in academia and consulting, which is an incredibly demanding environment, just constantly pushing yourself, constantly under pressure to publish, to secure grants, to advise graduate students who were often in the middle of their own crises. It was a pressure cooker, frankly. And of course, there were times during all those years when things were difficult. When a major project would fall through, or when my late wife and I were first married and struggling to make ends meet while I finished my dissertation—those were stressful times. Very stressful. \n\nBut a period of two straight weeks where I felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day? Where it just blanketed everything? I really... I don't think so. I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that high-energy period, did you hear or see things that other people could not?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hear or see things? Like... hallucinations, you mean? Oh, heavens no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, my mind was racing a mile a minute, certainly, and I was making connections that felt incredibly profound—like I said about the market patterns and the arts council—but I was always firmly planted in reality. 

I wasn't seeing visions or hearing voices whispering to me from the corners of the room. It wasn't anything mystical or... or psychotic, if that's the word. It was just pure, unadulterated intellectual energy. The things I was seeing were on paper, in spreadsheets, in organizational structures. I was seeing solutions, doctor, not... not phantoms. I've spent my entire life dealing in empirical data, in research and observable facts, and even when I was feeling so incredibly energized and powerful, that didn't change. I was just processing the facts much, much faster than anyone else. But no, no imaginary sights or sounds. Just a very loud, very fast stream of my own thoughts.

I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you hear or see things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Hear or see things? Like... hallucinations, you mean? Oh, heavens no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, my mind was racing a mile a minute, certainly, and I was making connections that felt incredibly profound—like I said about the market patterns and the arts council—but I was always firmly planted in reality. \n\nI wasn't seeing visions or hearing voices whispering to me from the corners of the room. It wasn't anything mystical or... or psychotic, if that's the word. It was just pure, unadulterated intellectual energy. The things I was seeing were on paper, in spreadsheets, in organizational structures. I was seeing solutions, doctor, not... not phantoms. I've spent my entire life dealing in empirical data, in research and observable facts, and even when I was feeling so incredibly energized and powerful, that didn't change. I was just processing the facts much, much faster than anyone else. But no, no imaginary sights or sounds. Just a very loud, very fast stream of my own thoughts.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that high-energy period, did you believe anything about yourself or your abilities that other people told you was clearly untrue?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Untrue? Well, you see, doctor, "untrue" is a remarkably subjective term, especially when you're dealing with people who simply lack the capacity to see the bigger picture. My daughter, for instance, and a few of the more... let's say, conservative members of the boards I sit on, certainly used words like "impossible" or "unrealistic." But I didn't see it that way at all. I mean, after forty years in academia and consulting, you develop a certain analytical acumen, but during those weeks, it was as if my intellect had been completely supercharged. I truly, deeply believed that my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s. It wasn't arrogance, it was just... an undeniable clarity. 

For example, I looked at my retirement accounts—which, frankly, my broker had been mismanaging for years, just playing it far too safe—and I saw exactly, mathematically, how to repair them and completely outpace the market. It was so obvious to me, the patterns were just leaping off the screen. And while I was restructuring my entire financial portfolio, I also decided I had the bandwidth to advise three different tech start-ups simultaneously. Three of them! I had read about their supply chain issues in a trade journal, and I just knew precisely how to fix their operational bottlenecks. I was drafting these incredibly detailed, frankly brilliant proposals for their CEOs in the middle of the night and firing off emails. 

And it wasn't just finance and tech. I've been on the local arts council for a few years—usually just a tedious exercise in approving small grants for community theater—but I suddenly realized I could turn the whole organization into a massive regional powerhouse. I drew up a complete structural overhaul, integrating international partnerships, multi-million dollar endowments, a complete rebranding... it was visionary. 

And when I presented this to the committee, and when I told my family about the start-ups and the market trades, they looked at me as if I had grown a second head. They told me I was entirely out of touch with reality, that I couldn't possibly do all of these things, especially at sixty-nine years old. They said it was impossible. But I genuinely believed I could do it all without breaking a sweat. I felt like I was operating on a completely different intellectual plane, and they were all just dragging their feet, unable to comprehend the sheer scale of what I was capable of achieving. So, they called it untrue, but to me, it was just the logical next step in my career.

I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you hear or see things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Hear or see things? Like... hallucinations, you mean? Oh, heavens no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, my mind was racing a mile a minute, certainly, and I was making connections that felt incredibly profound—like I said about the market patterns and the arts council—but I was always firmly planted in reality. \n\nI wasn't seeing visions or hearing voices whispering to me from the corners of the room. It wasn't anything mystical or... or psychotic, if that's the word. It was just pure, unadulterated intellectual energy. The things I was seeing were on paper, in spreadsheets, in organizational structures. I was seeing solutions, doctor, not... not phantoms. I've spent my entire life dealing in empirical data, in research and observable facts, and even when I was feeling so incredibly energized and powerful, that didn't change. I was just processing the facts much, much faster than anyone else. But no, no imaginary sights or sounds. Just a very loud, very fast stream of my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe anything about yourself or your abilities that other people told you was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Untrue? Well, you see, doctor, \"untrue\" is a remarkably subjective term, especially when you're dealing with people who simply lack the capacity to see the bigger picture. My daughter, for instance, and a few of the more... let's say, conservative members of the boards I sit on, certainly used words like \"impossible\" or \"unrealistic.\" But I didn't see it that way at all. I mean, after forty years in academia and consulting, you develop a certain analytical acumen, but during those weeks, it was as if my intellect had been completely supercharged. I truly, deeply believed that my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s. It wasn't arrogance, it was just... an undeniable clarity. \n\nFor example, I looked at my retirement accounts—which, frankly, my broker had been mismanaging for years, just playing it far too safe—and I saw exactly, mathematically, how to repair them and completely outpace the market. It was so obvious to me, the patterns were just leaping off the screen. And while I was restructuring my entire financial portfolio, I also decided I had the bandwidth to advise three different tech start-ups simultaneously. Three of them! I had read about their supply chain issues in a trade journal, and I just knew precisely how to fix their operational bottlenecks. I was drafting these incredibly detailed, frankly brilliant proposals for their CEOs in the middle of the night and firing off emails. \n\nAnd it wasn't just finance and tech. I've been on the local arts council for a few years—usually just a tedious exercise in approving small grants for community theater—but I suddenly realized I could turn the whole organization into a massive regional powerhouse. I drew up a complete structural overhaul, integrating international partnerships, multi-million dollar endowments, a complete rebranding... it was visionary. \n\nAnd when I presented this to the committee, and when I told my family about the start-ups and the market trades, they looked at me as if I had grown a second head. They told me I was entirely out of touch with reality, that I couldn't possibly do all of these things, especially at sixty-nine years old. They said it was impossible. But I genuinely believed I could do it all without breaking a sweat. I felt like I was operating on a completely different intellectual plane, and they were all just dragging their feet, unable to comprehend the sheer scale of what I was capable of achieving. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Another time? No, no, I really don't think so. I mean, as I mentioned before, I've always been a high-energy individual. You don't survive forty years in academia, juggling teaching loads, committee work, and the relentless pressure to publish, without a certain... robust constitution, shall we say. I was the sort of professor who was always the first one in the department building and the last one out. I prided myself on my stamina. And yes, there were certainly times, particularly when a book manuscript was due or a major grant proposal was in its final stages, where I would burn the midnight oil. I might sleep four or five hours a night for a week or two just to get the work done. 

But that was always driven by external deadlines, you see? It was a necessity of the job, a calculated decision to push myself to achieve a specific goal. And once the deadline passed, I would inevitably crash, sleep for twelve hours straight, and go back to a normal routine. It was exhausting, frankly. 

This recent episode... this was entirely different. This wasn't driven by a deadline; it was driven by this internal, inexhaustible engine that suddenly switched on. I didn't *need* sleep, and I didn't want it, because sleep felt like an absolute waste of time when there were so many brilliant ideas to execute. I had never experienced anything like that before, not even in my twenties when I was supposedly at my physical peak. To feel that level of electricity, that absolute lack of fatigue, day after day, without any external pressure forcing me to stay awake... no, that is completely unprecedented in my sixty-nine years. It was a completely novel experience. So, looking back, I can confidently say I've never had a period quite like this recent one.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF30.2, F31.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, to be perfectly honest, it’s my sleep. It has become completely unmoored. Just utterly untethered from reality. At first, I actually thought it was a good thing, you know? I’ve been out of the workforce for a while now, but suddenly, a few weeks ago, I was waking up after only two or three hours of sleep, and I wasn't tired in the slightest. I felt incredibly sharp, just completely ready to go. I’d get out of bed at two or three in the morning, go straight to my study, and just start drafting these elaborate plans—pulling out my old graduate school notes, outlining huge projects, writing pages and pages until the sun came up. I felt like I had this incredible new lease on life, to be frank. \n\nBut... well, that’s not what’s happening now. It’s shifted into something else entirely. Now, I still can't sleep, but that productive energy is completely gone. I just lie awake in bed, hours before dawn, and my stomach is so tight, just tied in these horrible knots. I toss and I turn, and there is absolutely no sense of rest whatsoever. I’m physically exhausted, bone-tired really, but I just lie there in the dark feeling completely wretched and tense. It's just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore, and the nights just feel impossibly long.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually powerful, gifted, or important?\n\nPatient: Powerful? Gifted? Well, it’s funny you use those exact words, doctor, because... yes. Absolutely. I mean, sitting here in this chair right now, exhausted and miserable, it sounds completely absurd, but at the time, the clarity I possessed was just staggering. It was as if a fog I didn't even know was there had completely lifted. \n\nI would be sitting in my study at three in the morning, looking over my portfolios, and I truly believed—no, I *knew*—that I could single-handedly repair my retirement accounts, which, let me tell you, took quite a hit over the last few years with all the market fluctuations. I saw patterns in the market that I thought everyone else was just too blind to see. But it wasn't just my own finances. I had this absolute certainty that I was the missing piece for other people, too. I started drafting these incredibly lengthy emails to three different tech start-ups—companies I have absolutely no prior relationship with, mind you, just places I had read about—convinced I was the only person alive who could properly advise them on their expansion strategies. \n\nAnd the local arts council! I've been a passive, quiet donor for a decade, never really involved, but suddenly I had this grand, sweeping vision to turn the entire organization into a regional powerhouse. I was drawing up organizational charts, drafting new mission statements, completely restructuring their board of directors on my legal pads. I felt... well, I felt as though my judgment was suddenly, undeniably superior to everyone else’s. Like I had tapped into this well of sheer genius that had just been sitting dormant since my graduate school days, and I was the only one who could see how all the pieces of the world were supposed to fit together. I was going to fix everything, doctor, just completely revolutionize all of it...\n\nDoctor: Before this energized period began, had you ever gone through a stretch where you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, you know, doctor, it's interesting you ask about that timeframe specifically, because when I think back to the months before this whole... explosion of energy happened, it's almost hard to reconcile the two periods. But depressed most of the day, nearly every day? No, I wouldn't say that. Not at all, really. \n\nI mean, certainly, being out of the workforce at my age, having stepped away from the daily grind and the intellectual stimulation of my career, there are moments of quiet. There are afternoons where the house feels a bit too still, or days where I might wonder if I retired too early, or if I should have taken on that consulting role they offered me a few years back. You know, you spend decades building a career, pouring your intellect into complex problems, and then suddenly you're just... tending to the garden or reading the paper. It's an adjustment, undoubtedly. There were days where I felt perhaps a bit aimless, or maybe just bored, if I'm being perfectly frank. But a deep, pervasive depression? No. \n\nI wasn't spending my days crying or unable to get out of bed, nothing like that. I was still going to my weekly lunch with my old colleagues—well, most weeks anyway, unless the weather was terrible or my arthritis was acting up. I was still keeping up with the financial news, reading my journals, doing the crossword. I was functioning perfectly fine, just perhaps living a quieter, less vibrant life than I used to. So, no, I wouldn't classify my mood before this recent... episode as depressed. It was just an ordinary, perhaps slightly mundane, retirement existence before everything suddenly shifted into high gear.\n\nDoctor: At any other time in your life, have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Hmm. You know, doctor, looking back over the entire span of my life... and at sixty-nine, there's quite a bit of life to look back on, isn't there? I mean, I spent over forty years in academia and consulting, which is an incredibly demanding environment, just constantly pushing yourself, constantly under pressure to publish, to secure grants, to advise graduate students who were often in the middle of their own crises. It was a pressure cooker, frankly. And of course, there were times during all those years when things were difficult. When a major project would fall through, or when my late wife and I were first married and struggling to make ends meet while I finished my dissertation—those were stressful times. Very stressful. \n\nBut a period of two straight weeks where I felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day? Where it just blanketed everything? I really... I don't think so. I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you hear or see things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Hear or see things? Like... hallucinations, you mean? Oh, heavens no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, my mind was racing a mile a minute, certainly, and I was making connections that felt incredibly profound—like I said about the market patterns and the arts council—but I was always firmly planted in reality. \n\nI wasn't seeing visions or hearing voices whispering to me from the corners of the room. It wasn't anything mystical or... or psychotic, if that's the word. It was just pure, unadulterated intellectual energy. The things I was seeing were on paper, in spreadsheets, in organizational structures. I was seeing solutions, doctor, not... not phantoms. I've spent my entire life dealing in empirical data, in research and observable facts, and even when I was feeling so incredibly energized and powerful, that didn't change. I was just processing the facts much, much faster than anyone else. But no, no imaginary sights or sounds. Just a very loud, very fast stream of my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe anything about yourself or your abilities that other people told you was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Untrue? Well, you see, doctor, \"untrue\" is a remarkably subjective term, especially when you're dealing with people who simply lack the capacity to see the bigger picture. My daughter, for instance, and a few of the more... let's say, conservative members of the boards I sit on, certainly used words like \"impossible\" or \"unrealistic.\" But I didn't see it that way at all. I mean, after forty years in academia and consulting, you develop a certain analytical acumen, but during those weeks, it was as if my intellect had been completely supercharged. I truly, deeply believed that my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s. It wasn't arrogance, it was just... an undeniable clarity. \n\nFor example, I looked at my retirement accounts—which, frankly, my broker had been mismanaging for years, just playing it far too safe—and I saw exactly, mathematically, how to repair them and completely outpace the market. It was so obvious to me, the patterns were just leaping off the screen. And while I was restructuring my entire financial portfolio, I also decided I had the bandwidth to advise three different tech start-ups simultaneously. Three of them! I had read about their supply chain issues in a trade journal, and I just knew precisely how to fix their operational bottlenecks. I was drafting these incredibly detailed, frankly brilliant proposals for their CEOs in the middle of the night and firing off emails. \n\nAnd it wasn't just finance and tech. I've been on the local arts council for a few years—usually just a tedious exercise in approving small grants for community theater—but I suddenly realized I could turn the whole organization into a massive regional powerhouse. I drew up a complete structural overhaul, integrating international partnerships, multi-million dollar endowments, a complete rebranding... it was visionary. \n\nAnd when I presented this to the committee, and when I told my family about the start-ups and the market trades, they looked at me as if I had grown a second head. They told me I was entirely out of touch with reality, that I couldn't possibly do all of these things, especially at sixty-nine years old. They said it was impossible. But I genuinely believed I could do it all without breaking a sweat. I felt like I was operating on a completely different intellectual plane, and they were all just dragging their feet, unable to comprehend the sheer scale of what I was capable of achieving. So, they called it untrue, but to me, it was just the logical next step in my career.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent high-energy period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt similarly unusually energized or needed far less sleep?\n\nPatient: Another time? No, no, I really don't think so. I mean, as I mentioned before, I've always been a high-energy individual. You don't survive forty years in academia, juggling teaching loads, committee work, and the relentless pressure to publish, without a certain... robust constitution, shall we say. I was the sort of professor who was always the first one in the department building and the last one out. I prided myself on my stamina. And yes, there were certainly times, particularly when a book manuscript was due or a major grant proposal was in its final stages, where I would burn the midnight oil. I might sleep four or five hours a night for a week or two just to get the work done. \n\nBut that was always driven by external deadlines, you see? It was a necessity of the job, a calculated decision to push myself to achieve a specific goal. And once the deadline passed, I would inevitably crash, sleep for twelve hours straight, and go back to a normal routine. It was exhausting, frankly. \n\nThis recent episode... this was entirely different. This wasn't driven by a deadline; it was driven by this internal, inexhaustible engine that suddenly switched on. I didn't *need* sleep, and I didn't want it, because sleep felt like an absolute waste of time when there were so many brilliant ideas to execute. I had never experienced anything like that before, not even in my twenties when I was supposedly at my physical peak. To feel that level of electricity, that absolute lack of fatigue, day after day, without any external pressure forcing me to stay awake... no, that is completely unprecedented in my sixty-nine years. It was a completely novel experience. So, looking back, I can confidently say I've never had a period quite like this recent one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

You don't survive forty years in academia, juggling teaching loads, committee work, and the relentless pressure to publish, without a certain... robust constitution, shall we say. I was the sort of professor who was always the first one in the department building and the last one out. I prided myself on my stamina. And yes, there were certainly times, particularly when a book manuscript was due or a major grant proposal was in its final stages, where I would burn the midnight oil. I might sleep four or five hours a night for a week or two just to get the work done. \n\nBut that was always driven by external deadlines, you see? It was a necessity of the job, a calculated decision to push myself to achieve a specific goal. And once the deadline passed, I would inevitably crash, sleep for twelve hours straight, and go back to a normal routine. It was exhausting, frankly. \n\nThis recent episode... this was entirely different. This wasn't driven by a deadline; it was driven by this internal, inexhaustible engine that suddenly switched on. I didn't *need* sleep, and I didn't want it, because sleep felt like an absolute waste of time when there were so many brilliant ideas to execute. I had never experienced anything like that before, not even in my twenties when I was supposedly at my physical peak. To feel that level of electricity, that absolute lack of fatigue, day after day, without any external pressure forcing me to stay awake... no, that is completely unprecedented in my sixty-nine years. It was a completely novel experience. So, looking back, I can confidently say I've never had a period quite like this recent one.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of your behavior or mental state?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hospitalized? Good heavens, no. No, no, no. I mean, the very idea of it is... well, it's frankly absurd. I was sitting in my own study, in my own home, surrounded by my books and my files, doing what I have done my entire professional life—analyzing data and solving complex problems. I wasn't running through the streets or... or causing a public disturbance. 

Now, I will grant you, my daughter was terribly distressed. She came over one evening—it must have been a Tuesday, because she usually comes for dinner on Tuesdays—and she found me at my desk with papers spread all over the floor, three computer monitors going at once, and I was in the middle of a rather intense phone call with an associate in Tokyo regarding one of those start-ups I mentioned. She started insisting that I needed to see someone, that I was "manic," which is a word she threw around with absolutely no clinical understanding, I might add. She even threatened to call an ambulance at one point, which I found deeply insulting. An ambulance! For a man who was simply working diligently on restructuring his portfolio and advising emerging businesses. 

I told her, I said, "Eleanor, if you call an ambulance, I will simply explain to the paramedics that I am conducting high-level consulting work and they will leave." And she didn't call, of course, because she knew I was right. I was entirely cogent, entirely rational, just... highly productive. So no, there was no hospital involved. The thought of being locked away in some... some ward, surrounded by people who are genuinely unwell, while I was in the midst of the most intellectually fertile period of my late career... it's just laughable. No, I remained at home, at my desk, where I belonged.

Like I had tapped into this well of sheer genius that had just been sitting dormant since my graduate school days, and I was the only one who could see how all the pieces of the world were supposed to fit together. I was going to fix everything, doctor, just completely revolutionize all of it...\n\nDoctor: Before this energized period began, had you ever gone through a stretch where you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, you know, doctor, it's interesting you ask about that timeframe specifically, because when I think back to the months before this whole... explosion of energy happened, it's almost hard to reconcile the two periods. But depressed most of the day, nearly every day? No, I wouldn't say that. Not at all, really. \n\nI mean, certainly, being out of the workforce at my age, having stepped away from the daily grind and the intellectual stimulation of my career, there are moments of quiet. There are afternoons where the house feels a bit too still, or days where I might wonder if I retired too early, or if I should have taken on that consulting role they offered me a few years back. You know, you spend decades building a career, pouring your intellect into complex problems, and then suddenly you're just... tending to the garden or reading the paper. It's an adjustment, undoubtedly. There were days where I felt perhaps a bit aimless, or maybe just bored, if I'm being perfectly frank. But a deep, pervasive depression? No. \n\nI wasn't spending my days crying or unable to get out of bed, nothing like that. I was still going to my weekly lunch with my old colleagues—well, most weeks anyway, unless the weather was terrible or my arthritis was acting up. I was still keeping up with the financial news, reading my journals, doing the crossword. I was functioning perfectly fine, just perhaps living a quieter, less vibrant life than I used to. So, no, I wouldn't classify my mood before this recent... episode as depressed. It was just an ordinary, perhaps slightly mundane, retirement existence before everything suddenly shifted into high gear.\n\nDoctor: At any other time in your life, have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Hmm. You know, doctor, looking back over the entire span of my life... and at sixty-nine, there's quite a bit of life to look back on, isn't there? I mean, I spent over forty years in academia and consulting, which is an incredibly demanding environment, just constantly pushing yourself, constantly under pressure to publish, to secure grants, to advise graduate students who were often in the middle of their own crises. It was a pressure cooker, frankly. And of course, there were times during all those years when things were difficult. When a major project would fall through, or when my late wife and I were first married and struggling to make ends meet while I finished my dissertation—those were stressful times. Very stressful. \n\nBut a period of two straight weeks where I felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day? Where it just blanketed everything? I really... I don't think so. I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you hear or see things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Hear or see things? Like... hallucinations, you mean? Oh, heavens no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, my mind was racing a mile a minute, certainly, and I was making connections that felt incredibly profound—like I said about the market patterns and the arts council—but I was always firmly planted in reality. \n\nI wasn't seeing visions or hearing voices whispering to me from the corners of the room. It wasn't anything mystical or... or psychotic, if that's the word. It was just pure, unadulterated intellectual energy. The things I was seeing were on paper, in spreadsheets, in organizational structures. I was seeing solutions, doctor, not... not phantoms. I've spent my entire life dealing in empirical data, in research and observable facts, and even when I was feeling so incredibly energized and powerful, that didn't change. I was just processing the facts much, much faster than anyone else. But no, no imaginary sights or sounds. Just a very loud, very fast stream of my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe anything about yourself or your abilities that other people told you was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Untrue? Well, you see, doctor, \"untrue\" is a remarkably subjective term, especially when you're dealing with people who simply lack the capacity to see the bigger picture. My daughter, for instance, and a few of the more... let's say, conservative members of the boards I sit on, certainly used words like \"impossible\" or \"unrealistic.\" But I didn't see it that way at all. I mean, after forty years in academia and consulting, you develop a certain analytical acumen, but during those weeks, it was as if my intellect had been completely supercharged. I truly, deeply believed that my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s. It wasn't arrogance, it was just... an undeniable clarity. \n\nFor example, I looked at my retirement accounts—which, frankly, my broker had been mismanaging for years, just playing it far too safe—and I saw exactly, mathematically, how to repair them and completely outpace the market. It was so obvious to me, the patterns were just leaping off the screen. And while I was restructuring my entire financial portfolio, I also decided I had the bandwidth to advise three different tech start-ups simultaneously. Three of them! I had read about their supply chain issues in a trade journal, and I just knew precisely how to fix their operational bottlenecks. I was drafting these incredibly detailed, frankly brilliant proposals for their CEOs in the middle of the night and firing off emails. \n\nAnd it wasn't just finance and tech. I've been on the local arts council for a few years—usually just a tedious exercise in approving small grants for community theater—but I suddenly realized I could turn the whole organization into a massive regional powerhouse. I drew up a complete structural overhaul, integrating international partnerships, multi-million dollar endowments, a complete rebranding... it was visionary. \n\nAnd when I presented this to the committee, and when I told my family about the start-ups and the market trades, they looked at me as if I had grown a second head. They told me I was entirely out of touch with reality, that I couldn't possibly do all of these things, especially at sixty-nine years old. They said it was impossible. But I genuinely believed I could do it all without breaking a sweat. I felt like I was operating on a completely different intellectual plane, and they were all just dragging their feet, unable to comprehend the sheer scale of what I was capable of achieving. So, they called it untrue, but to me, it was just the logical next step in my career.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent high-energy period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt similarly unusually energized or needed far less sleep?\n\nPatient: Another time? No, no, I really don't think so. I mean, as I mentioned before, I've always been a high-energy individual. You don't survive forty years in academia, juggling teaching loads, committee work, and the relentless pressure to publish, without a certain... robust constitution, shall we say. I was the sort of professor who was always the first one in the department building and the last one out. I prided myself on my stamina. And yes, there were certainly times, particularly when a book manuscript was due or a major grant proposal was in its final stages, where I would burn the midnight oil. I might sleep four or five hours a night for a week or two just to get the work done. \n\nBut that was always driven by external deadlines, you see? It was a necessity of the job, a calculated decision to push myself to achieve a specific goal. And once the deadline passed, I would inevitably crash, sleep for twelve hours straight, and go back to a normal routine. It was exhausting, frankly. \n\nThis recent episode... this was entirely different. This wasn't driven by a deadline; it was driven by this internal, inexhaustible engine that suddenly switched on. I didn't *need* sleep, and I didn't want it, because sleep felt like an absolute waste of time when there were so many brilliant ideas to execute. I had never experienced anything like that before, not even in my twenties when I was supposedly at my physical peak. To feel that level of electricity, that absolute lack of fatigue, day after day, without any external pressure forcing me to stay awake... no, that is completely unprecedented in my sixty-nine years. It was a completely novel experience. So, looking back, I can confidently say I've never had a period quite like this recent one.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of your behavior or mental state?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Good heavens, no. No, no, no. I mean, the very idea of it is... well, it's frankly absurd. I was sitting in my own study, in my own home, surrounded by my books and my files, doing what I have done my entire professional life—analyzing data and solving complex problems. I wasn't running through the streets or... or causing a public disturbance. \n\nNow, I will grant you, my daughter was terribly distressed. She came over one evening—it must have been a Tuesday, because she usually comes for dinner on Tuesdays—and she found me at my desk with papers spread all over the floor, three computer monitors going at once, and I was in the middle of a rather intense phone call with an associate in Tokyo regarding one of those start-ups I mentioned. She started insisting that I needed to see someone, that I was \"manic,\" which is a word she threw around with absolutely no clinical understanding, I might add. She even threatened to call an ambulance at one point, which I found deeply insulting. An ambulance! For a man who was simply working diligently on restructuring his portfolio and advising emerging businesses. \n\nI told her, I said, \"Eleanor, if you call an ambulance, I will simply explain to the paramedics that I am conducting high-level consulting work and they will leave.\" And she didn't call, of course, because she knew I was right. I was entirely cogent, entirely rational, just... highly productive. So no, there was no hospital involved. The thought of being locked away in some... some ward, surrounded by people who are genuinely unwell, while I was in the midst of the most intellectually fertile period of my late career... it's just laughable. No, I remained at home, at my desk, where I belonged.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No, I remained at home, at my desk, where I belonged.\n\n[Last Question]\nRight now, do you believe any of these ideas about your abilities are definitely true even though other people say they cannot be true?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into a regional powerhouse, as though my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Right now? Well, doctor, that is... it’s a rather complex question to answer with a simple yes or no, because you have to separate the validity of the ideas from the, shall we say, logistical reality of executing them. 

If you're asking me if I still think the strategies themselves are sound—the market analysis, the operational bottlenecks I identified for those companies—yes

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, to be perfectly honest, it’s my sleep. It has become completely unmoored. Just utterly untethered from reality. At first, I actually thought it was a good thing, you know? I’ve been out of the workforce for a while now, but suddenly, a few weeks ago, I was waking up after only two or three hours of sleep, and I wasn't tired in the slightest. I felt incredibly sharp, just completely ready to go. I’d get out of bed at two or three in the morning, go straight to my study, and just start drafting these elaborate plans—pulling out my old graduate school notes, outlining huge projects, writing pages and pages until the sun came up. I felt like I had this incredible new lease on life, to be frank. \n\nBut... well, that’s not what’s happening now. It’s shifted into something else entirely. Now, I still can't sleep, but that productive energy is completely gone. I just lie awake in bed, hours before dawn, and my stomach is so tight, just tied in these horrible knots. I toss and I turn, and there is absolutely no sense of rest whatsoever. I’m physically exhausted, bone-tired really, but I just lie there in the dark feeling completely wretched and tense. It's just... I don't know how to turn it off anymore, and the nights just feel impossibly long.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually powerful, gifted, or important?\n\nPatient: Powerful? Gifted? Well, it’s funny you use those exact words, doctor, because... yes. Absolutely. I mean, sitting here in this chair right now, exhausted and miserable, it sounds completely absurd, but at the time, the clarity I possessed was just staggering. It was as if a fog I didn't even know was there had completely lifted. \n\nI would be sitting in my study at three in the morning, looking over my portfolios, and I truly believed—no, I *knew*—that I could single-handedly repair my retirement accounts, which, let me tell you, took quite a hit over the last few years with all the market fluctuations. I saw patterns in the market that I thought everyone else was just too blind to see. But it wasn't just my own finances. I had this absolute certainty that I was the missing piece for other people, too. I started drafting these incredibly lengthy emails to three different tech start-ups—companies I have absolutely no prior relationship with, mind you, just places I had read about—convinced I was the only person alive who could properly advise them on their expansion strategies. \n\nAnd the local arts council! I've been a passive, quiet donor for a decade, never really involved, but suddenly I had this grand, sweeping vision to turn the entire organization into a regional powerhouse. I was drawing up organizational charts, drafting new mission statements, completely restructuring their board of directors on my legal pads. I felt... well, I felt as though my judgment was suddenly, undeniably superior to everyone else’s. Like I had tapped into this well of sheer genius that had just been sitting dormant since my graduate school days, and I was the only one who could see how all the pieces of the world were supposed to fit together. I was going to fix everything, doctor, just completely revolutionize all of it...\n\nDoctor: Before this energized period began, had you ever gone through a stretch where you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well, you know, doctor, it's interesting you ask about that timeframe specifically, because when I think back to the months before this whole... explosion of energy happened, it's almost hard to reconcile the two periods. But depressed most of the day, nearly every day? No, I wouldn't say that. Not at all, really. \n\nI mean, certainly, being out of the workforce at my age, having stepped away from the daily grind and the intellectual stimulation of my career, there are moments of quiet. There are afternoons where the house feels a bit too still, or days where I might wonder if I retired too early, or if I should have taken on that consulting role they offered me a few years back. You know, you spend decades building a career, pouring your intellect into complex problems, and then suddenly you're just... tending to the garden or reading the paper. It's an adjustment, undoubtedly. There were days where I felt perhaps a bit aimless, or maybe just bored, if I'm being perfectly frank. But a deep, pervasive depression? No. \n\nI wasn't spending my days crying or unable to get out of bed, nothing like that. I was still going to my weekly lunch with my old colleagues—well, most weeks anyway, unless the weather was terrible or my arthritis was acting up. I was still keeping up with the financial news, reading my journals, doing the crossword. I was functioning perfectly fine, just perhaps living a quieter, less vibrant life than I used to. So, no, I wouldn't classify my mood before this recent... episode as depressed. It was just an ordinary, perhaps slightly mundane, retirement existence before everything suddenly shifted into high gear.\n\nDoctor: At any other time in your life, have you ever gone through a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Hmm. You know, doctor, looking back over the entire span of my life... and at sixty-nine, there's quite a bit of life to look back on, isn't there? I mean, I spent over forty years in academia and consulting, which is an incredibly demanding environment, just constantly pushing yourself, constantly under pressure to publish, to secure grants, to advise graduate students who were often in the middle of their own crises. It was a pressure cooker, frankly. And of course, there were times during all those years when things were difficult. When a major project would fall through, or when my late wife and I were first married and struggling to make ends meet while I finished my dissertation—those were stressful times. Very stressful. \n\nBut a period of two straight weeks where I felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day? Where it just blanketed everything? I really... I don't think so. I've always been a rather pragmatic person, you see. A problem solver. When things got tough, my instinct was always to work harder, to analyze the situation, to find the structural flaw and fix it. I might have been stressed, I might have been exhausted—Lord knows I pulled enough all-nighters in my thirties to last a lifetime—but I wouldn't call it depression. I never lost my drive or my ability to function. Even when my wife passed, which was, of course, a profoundly sad time, a terrible grief... I still had my work. I threw myself into my research. It was a heavy sadness, certainly, but it wasn't that sort of pervasive, paralyzing depression where you just can't see the point of anything day after day. So, no, I really can't recall a specific two-week block in my life that fits that description. I've always just sort of... powered through, I suppose. Until this recent business with the sleep, of course, which is just entirely unprecedented for me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you hear or see things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Hear or see things? Like... hallucinations, you mean? Oh, heavens no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean, my mind was racing a mile a minute, certainly, and I was making connections that felt incredibly profound—like I said about the market patterns and the arts council—but I was always firmly planted in reality. \n\nI wasn't seeing visions or hearing voices whispering to me from the corners of the room. It wasn't anything mystical or... or psychotic, if that's the word. It was just pure, unadulterated intellectual energy. The things I was seeing were on paper, in spreadsheets, in organizational structures. I was seeing solutions, doctor, not... not phantoms. I've spent my entire life dealing in empirical data, in research and observable facts, and even when I was feeling so incredibly energized and powerful, that didn't change. I was just processing the facts much, much faster than anyone else. But no, no imaginary sights or sounds. Just a very loud, very fast stream of my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe anything about yourself or your abilities that other people told you was clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Untrue? Well, you see, doctor, \"untrue\" is a remarkably subjective term, especially when you're dealing with people who simply lack the capacity to see the bigger picture. My daughter, for instance, and a few of the more... let's say, conservative members of the boards I sit on, certainly used words like \"impossible\" or \"unrealistic.\" But I didn't see it that way at all. I mean, after forty years in academia and consulting, you develop a certain analytical acumen, but during those weeks, it was as if my intellect had been completely supercharged. I truly, deeply believed that my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s. It wasn't arrogance, it was just... an undeniable clarity. \n\nFor example, I looked at my retirement accounts—which, frankly, my broker had been mismanaging for years, just playing it far too safe—and I saw exactly, mathematically, how to repair them and completely outpace the market. It was so obvious to me, the patterns were just leaping off the screen. And while I was restructuring my entire financial portfolio, I also decided I had the bandwidth to advise three different tech start-ups simultaneously. Three of them! I had read about their supply chain issues in a trade journal, and I just knew precisely how to fix their operational bottlenecks. I was drafting these incredibly detailed, frankly brilliant proposals for their CEOs in the middle of the night and firing off emails. \n\nAnd it wasn't just finance and tech. I've been on the local arts council for a few years—usually just a tedious exercise in approving small grants for community theater—but I suddenly realized I could turn the whole organization into a massive regional powerhouse. I drew up a complete structural overhaul, integrating international partnerships, multi-million dollar endowments, a complete rebranding... it was visionary. \n\nAnd when I presented this to the committee, and when I told my family about the start-ups and the market trades, they looked at me as if I had grown a second head. They told me I was entirely out of touch with reality, that I couldn't possibly do all of these things, especially at sixty-nine years old. They said it was impossible. But I genuinely believed I could do it all without breaking a sweat. I felt like I was operating on a completely different intellectual plane, and they were all just dragging their feet, unable to comprehend the sheer scale of what I was capable of achieving. So, they called it untrue, but to me, it was just the logical next step in my career.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent high-energy period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt similarly unusually energized or needed far less sleep?\n\nPatient: Another time? No, no, I really don't think so. I mean, as I mentioned before, I've always been a high-energy individual. You don't survive forty years in academia, juggling teaching loads, committee work, and the relentless pressure to publish, without a certain... robust constitution, shall we say. I was the sort of professor who was always the first one in the department building and the last one out. I prided myself on my stamina. And yes, there were certainly times, particularly when a book manuscript was due or a major grant proposal was in its final stages, where I would burn the midnight oil. I might sleep four or five hours a night for a week or two just to get the work done. \n\nBut that was always driven by external deadlines, you see? It was a necessity of the job, a calculated decision to push myself to achieve a specific goal. And once the deadline passed, I would inevitably crash, sleep for twelve hours straight, and go back to a normal routine. It was exhausting, frankly. \n\nThis recent episode... this was entirely different. This wasn't driven by a deadline; it was driven by this internal, inexhaustible engine that suddenly switched on. I didn't *need* sleep, and I didn't want it, because sleep felt like an absolute waste of time when there were so many brilliant ideas to execute. I had never experienced anything like that before, not even in my twenties when I was supposedly at my physical peak. To feel that level of electricity, that absolute lack of fatigue, day after day, without any external pressure forcing me to stay awake... no, that is completely unprecedented in my sixty-nine years. It was a completely novel experience. So, looking back, I can confidently say I've never had a period quite like this recent one.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of your behavior or mental state?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Good heavens, no. No, no, no. I mean, the very idea of it is... well, it's frankly absurd. I was sitting in my own study, in my own home, surrounded by my books and my files, doing what I have done my entire professional life—analyzing data and solving complex problems. I wasn't running through the streets or... or causing a public disturbance. \n\nNow, I will grant you, my daughter was terribly distressed. She came over one evening—it must have been a Tuesday, because she usually comes for dinner on Tuesdays—and she found me at my desk with papers spread all over the floor, three computer monitors going at once, and I was in the middle of a rather intense phone call with an associate in Tokyo regarding one of those start-ups I mentioned. She started insisting that I needed to see someone, that I was \"manic,\" which is a word she threw around with absolutely no clinical understanding, I might add. She even threatened to call an ambulance at one point, which I found deeply insulting. An ambulance! For a man who was simply working diligently on restructuring his portfolio and advising emerging businesses. \n\nI told her, I said, \"Eleanor, if you call an ambulance, I will simply explain to the paramedics that I am conducting high-level consulting work and they will leave.\" And she didn't call, of course, because she knew I was right. I was entirely cogent, entirely rational, just... highly productive. So no, there was no hospital involved. The thought of being locked away in some... some ward, surrounded by people who are genuinely unwell, while I was in the midst of the most intellectually fertile period of my late career... it's just laughable. No, I remained at home, at my desk, where I belonged.\n\nDoctor: Right now, do you believe any of these ideas about your abilities are definitely true even though other people say they cannot be true?\n\nPatient: Right now? Well, doctor, that is... it’s a rather complex question to answer with a simple yes or no, because you have to separate the validity of the ideas from the, shall we say, logistical reality of executing them. \n\nIf you're asking me if I still think the strategies themselves are sound—the market analysis, the operational bottlenecks I identified for those companies—yes\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
